- 12 INTRODUCTION • Random Variable: A random variable (r.v.) is a quantity which depends on the outcome of the experiment. In mathematical terms, this is a map from the space Ω into some space E (and E is very often R, Rd ,N,Z) and this map X : Ω → E should be measurable. We always think of E as a measure space that is E is endowed with a σ-Algebra E, and from measure theory we know that one can transport P on E: for all B ∈ E, PX (or PX ) is a probability measure on E such that for all B ∈ E,   PX [B] = PX [B] = P[X −1 (B)] = P {ω ∈ Ω | X(ω) ∈ B} | {z } ∈A and PX is called the law or the distribution of X. Example 0.2. We got the following examples: (i) Toss of two dice: Ω = {(i, j) | 1 ≤ i ≤ 6, 1 ≤ j ≤ 6} A = P(Ω)(= 2Ω ) 1 ∀ω ∈ Ω, P[{ω}] = 36 |A| ∀A ⊂ Ω, P[A] = 36 The map Ω → (N, P(N)), (i, j) 7→ i + j is a r.v. for all B ⊂ N, |{(i, j) | i + j ∈ B}| PX [B] = . 36 1 For instance if B = {2} then PX [{2}] = P[{1, 1}] = 36 . To conclude the introduction, let us emphasize the fact that Ω can be more involved and the construction of P "completed". (ii) We throw a die until we get a 6. Here the choice of Ω is less obvious. The number of time, we have to throw the die, is not bounded. A natural choice for Ω would be × Ω = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}N , ω ∈ Ω that is ω = (ω1 , ..., ωn , ...) with ωj = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} for all j ≥ 1. A will be the product σ-Algebra i.e. the smallest σ-Algebra containing all sets of the form {ω | ω1 = i1 , ..., ωn = in }, n ≥ 1, i1 , ..., in ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} and P is the unique probability measure on Ω. (iii) We are interested in the motion of a particle in the space, and this particle is subject to some random perturbations. If the time interval is [0, 1] a natural space of outcomes can be the space of continuous functions from [0, 1] into Rd and we set Ω = C([0, 1], R3 ), ω ∈ Ω, is a possible trajectory of the particle: ω : [0, 1] → R3 . Take A to be the Borel σ-Algebra when we consider the supremumnorm on Ω kωk∞ = supt∈[0,1] |ω(t)| . A famous example for P is the so called Wiener measure (denoted by W). Under this measure, a typical trajectory is a Brownian motion.  <!-- lvl=1 i=21 sid=body[1]/p[22] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Definition 2.1 (Probability measure). Let (Ω, A) be a measurable space. A probability measure defined on A is an application P : A → [0, 1], such that (i) P[Ω] = 1. (ii) for all (An )n∈N , with An ∩ Am = ∅ for n 6= m, we have " # [ X P An = P[An ]. n∈N n∈N Remark 2.2. We want to recall the following properties without a proof. • For A, B ∈ A with A ⊂ B we get P[A] ≤ P[b]. • For A ∈ A we get P[AC ] = 1 − P[A]. • If (An )n∈N ⊂ A and An ↑ A as n → ∞, then P[An ] ↑ P[A] as n → ∞. • If (An )n∈N ⊂ A and An ↓ A as n → ∞, then P[An ] ↓ P[A] as n → ∞. Theorem 2.3. Let µ be a measure on a measureable space (Ω, A). (i) If (An )n∈N ⊂ A is an increasing family of measurable sets, then ! [ µ An = lim µ(An ). n→∞ n∈N Conversely, if an additive function satisfies the property above, then it is a measure. (ii) Let µ be an additive, positive and bounded function on A. Then σ-additivity is equivalent to say that for all decreasing sequences (Bn )n∈N ⊂ A, we get the implication \ Bn = ∅ =⇒ lim µ(Bn ) = 0. n→∞ n∈N 2.2. Probability measures on finite spaces. There are two cases where (Ω, A) is a measurable space, either Ω is finite or A is finite. An important question is how to define probability measures on finite or countable spaces. If I is at most countable, then having a probabilityPmeasure on P(I) is equivalent to having a family (ai )i∈I of positive real numbers such that i∈I ai = 1. Moreover, for A ⊂ I, we can define a measure µ by X µ(A) = ai , i∈A where µ({i}) = ai for all i ∈ I. Example 2.4. Theorem 2.6. Let A be a finite σ-Algebra on a state space Ω. Then there exists a partition A1 , ..., An of Ω, such that Ak ∈ A for all k ∈ {1, ..., n} and with the property that any A ∈ A can be written as a union of the events A1 , ..., An . The sets are called the atoms of A. T Proof. Fix ω ∈ Ω. Define the set C(ω) := B∈A, B. Then by finite intersection it follows ω∈B that C(ω) ∈ A. In fact C(ω) is the smallest element of A, which contains ω. Moreover, it is easy to see that ω ∼ ω 0 if and only if C(ω) = C(ω 0 ) defines an equivalence relation on Ω and that ω 0 ∈ C(ω) if and only if C(ω) = C(ω 0 ). Hence if A is an equivalence class and ω ∈ A, then A = C(ω). Therefore, one can take the events A1 , ..., An as the equivalence classes. 3. Basics of combinatorial Analysis 3.1. Sampling. Let us consider a population of size n ∈ N, i.e. a set S = {S1 , ..., Sn } with n elements. We call any ordered sequence (Si1 , ..., Sir ) of r elements of S a sample of size r, drawn from this population. Then there are two possible procedures. (i) Sampling with replacement, i.e. the same element can be drawn more than once. (ii) Sampling without replacement. Here an element one choses is removed from the population. In this case r ≤ n. In either case, our experiment is described by a sample space Ω in which each individual points represents a sample of size r. In the first case, we get |Ω| = nr , where an element ω ∈ Ω is of the form ω = (Si1 , ..., Sir ). In the second case we get n! |Ω| = n(n − 1) · · · (n − r + 1) = . (n − r)! Example 3.1. We got the following examples: (i) If by a "ten letter word" is meant a (possibly meaningless) sequence of ten letters, then such a word represents a sample from the population of 26 letters. There are 2610 such words. (ii) Tossing a coin r times is one way of obtaining a sample of size r drawn from the population of the letters H and T . Usually we assign equal probabilities to all samples, namely n−r is 1 sampling with replacement and n(n−1)...(n−r+1) is a sampling without replacement. (iii) A random sample of size r with replacement is taken from a population of size n, and we assume that r ≤ n. The probability p that in the sample no element appears twice is n(n − 1) · · · (n − r + 1) |A| p= P[A] = . nr |Ω|  <!-- lvl=1 i=27 sid=body[1]/p[28] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Example 3.4. A throw of twelve dice can result in 612 different outcomes. The event that each face can appear twice is 12! . 2 · 612 6 The case with replacement: We take the point of view of occupancy problems. Our model is that of placing randomly r balls into n cells. Such an event is completely described by its occu- pancy numbers r1 , ..., rn where rk stands for the number of balls in the k-th cell. Every n-tupel of integers satisfying r1 + ... + rk = r describes a possible configuration. Two distributions are distinguishable if the occupancy numbers are different. We denote by Ar,n the number of distin- guishable distributions. It is also the number of subpopulations of size r with replacement from a population S = {S1 , ..., Sn } of size n and it is characterized by the number rk of appearances of the individual Sk with the restriction r1 + ... + rn = r. Such lines will be called a path. We shall use (t, x) for coordinates. Definition 4.1. Let n > 0 and x be integers. A path (S0 , S1 , ..., Sn ) from the origin to the point (n, x) is a polygonal line whose vertices have abscissas 0, 1, 2, ..., n and ordinates S0 , S1 , ..., Sn satisfying (10) with Sn = x. We shall refer to n as the length of the path. There are 2n paths of length n. Moreover, we have n=p+q x=p−q A path from the origin to an arbitrary point (n, x) exists only if n and x are of the form as in the definition. In this case the p places for the positive k ’s can be chosen from the n = p + q available places in p+q p+q Nn,x = = p q different ways. Convention : Nn,x = 0 whenever n and x are not of the form as in the definition. this implies that, Nn,x represents the number of different paths from the origin to an arbitrary point (n, x). Example 4.2 (Ballot theorem). Suppose that in a ballot candidate P scores p votes and candidate Q scores q votes, where p > q. the probability that throughout the counting there are always more p−q votes for P than for Q equals p+q . The whole voting record may be represented by a path of length p + q in which k = +1, if the k’th vote is for P and k = −1 otherwise. Conversely every path from the origin to the point (p + q, p − q) can be interpreted as a voting with the given totals p and q. Sk is the number of votes by which P leads just after the k’th vote. The candidate P leads throughout the voting if S1 > 0, S2 > 0, ..., Sn > 0 (in the ballot theorem, it is implicitly assumed that all paths are equally probable). Let A = (a, α) and B = (b, β) be two independent points with b > a ≥ 0, a, b ∈ N, α > 0, β > 0, α, β ∈ N. By reflection of A on the t-axis we mean the point A0 = (a, −α). Lemma 4.3 (The reflection principle). The number of paths from A to B which touch or cross the t-axis equals the number of paths from A0 to B. Proof. Consider a path (Sa = α, Sa+1 , ..., Sb = B) from A to B having one or more vertices on that axis. Let t be the abscissa of the first such vertex that is Sa > 0, Sa+1 > 0, ..., Sk−1 > 0, Sk > 0. Then (−Sa , −Sa+1 , ..., −Sk−1 , Sk , Sk+1 , ..., Sb ) is a path leading from A0 to B and  <!-- lvl=1 i=37 sid=body[1]/p[38] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

The probability that up to time 2n, the last visit to the origin occurs at 2k is α2k,2n = U2k N2n−2k , k = 0, 1, ..., n Proof. We are concerned with paths satisfying S2k = 0, S2k+1 6= 0, ..., S2n 6= 0. The first 2k vertices of such paths can be chosen in 22k N2k different ways. Taking the point (2k, 0) as new origin and using the last lemma, we see that the next (2n − k) vertices can be chosen in 22n−2k N2n−2k different ways. Therefore we get 1 α2k,2n = 2n 22k N2k 22n−2k N2n−2k = U2k N2n−2k . 2  <!-- lvl=1 i=43 sid=body[1]/p[44] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Classical Probability distributions Let (Ω, A, P) denote a probability space and let X : (Ω, A, P) → (E, E) be a r.v. taking values in some measureable space (E, E). 2.1. Discrete distributions. 2.1.1. The uniform distribution. Let |E| < ∞. A r.v. X with values in E is said to be uniform on E if ∀x ∈ E 1 P[X = x] = . |E| 2.1.2. The Bernoulli distribution with parameter p ∈ [0, 1]. This is a r.v. X with values in {0, 1} such that P[X = 1] = p, P[X = 0] = 1 − p. The r.v. X can be interpreted as the outcome of a coin toss. The expectation of X is then given by E[X] = 0 · P[X = 0] + 1 · P[X = 1]. 2.1.3. The Binomial distribution B(n, p), n ∈ N, n ≥ 1, p ∈ [0, 1]. This is the distribution of a r.v. X taking its values in {0, 1, ..., n} such that n k P[X = k] = p (1 − p)n−k . k The r.v. X is interpreted as the number of heads of the n tosses of the previous case. One has to check that its a probability distribution: n n X X n k P[X = k] = p (1 − p)n−k = (p + (1 − p))n = 1. k k=0 k=0  <!-- lvl=1 i=52 sid=body[1]/p[53] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

- 2. CLASSICAL PROBABILITY DISTRIBUTIONS 33 Therefore we get E[X] = m. One can show similarly that E[(X − m)2 ] = σ 2 . 2.3. The distribution function. Let X : Ω → R be a real valued r.v. The distribution function of X is the function FX : R → [0, 1], t 7→ FX (t) := P[X ≤ t] = PX [(−∞, t)]. We claim that FX is increasing and right continuous. Meaning that lim FX (t) = 0, lim FX (t) = 1 t→−∞ t→∞ We can thus write P[a ≤ X ≤ b] = FX (b) − FX (a− ) and thus P[a < X < b] = F (b− ) − FX (a), | {z } lim t→a FX (t) t<a Moreover, for a single value we get P[X = a] = FX (a) − FX (a− ), which is called the jump of the function FX . If X and Y are two r.v.’s, such that FX (t) = FY (t), then PX = PY (this is a consequence of the monotone class theorem). If F is an increasing and right continuous function, then the set A = {a ∈ R | F (a) 6= F (a− )} is at most countable. If PX is absolutely continuous, then PX [{a}] = P[X = a] = 0, which implies that for all a ∈ R we have FX (a) = FX (a− ) and hence FX is continuous. An alternative point of view is to say that, if P (x) is the density of of PX , then Z Z t FX (x) = PX [(−∞, t]] = 1(−∞,t] (x)P (x)dx = P (x)dx, R −∞ is a continuous function of t. 2.4. σ-Algebras generated by a Random Variable. Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let X be a r.v. taking values in (E, E), i.e. X : (Ω, A, P) → (E, E). The σ-Algebra generated by X, denoted by σ(X), is by definition the smallest σ-Algebra, which makes X measurable. So we have σ(X) = {A = X −1 (B) | B ∈ E}. Remark 2.1. One can of course extend this definition to the case of a family of r.v.’s Xi for i ∈ I, taking values in (Ei , Ei ). In this case we have σ((Xi )i∈I ) = σ({Xi−1 (B) | Bi ∈ Ei , i ∈ I}). Proposition 2.2. Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let X be a r.v. with values in a measure space (E, E) and let Y be a real valued r.v. Then the following are equivalent. (i) Y is σ(X)-measurable. (ii) There exists a measurable map f : (E, E) → (R, B(R)), such that Y = f (X). Proof. So we have the following cases X : (Ω, A, P) −→ (E, E), X : (Ω, σ(X), P) −→ (E, E), Y : (Ω, A, P) −→ (R, B(R)) • (2) =⇒ (1): This follows from the fact that the composition of two measurable maps is measurable.  <!-- lvl=1 i=62 sid=body[1]/p[63] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Moments of Random Variables 3.1. Moments and Variance. Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let X be a r.v. and let p ≥ 1 be an integer (or even a real number). The p-th moment of X is by definition E[X p ], which is well defined when X ≥ 0 or E[|X|p ] < ∞, which is by definition Z p E[|X| ] = |X(ω)|p dP(ω) < ∞. Ω When p = 1, we get the expected value. We say that X is centered if E[X] = 0. The spaces Lp (Ω, A, P) for p ∈ [1, ∞) are defined as we have seen in the course M easure and Integral. From Hölder’s inequality we can observe that 1 1 E[|XY |] ≤ E[|X|p ] p E[|Y |q ] q , whenever p, q ≥ 1 and p1 + 1q = 1. If we take Y = 1 above, we obtain 1 E[X] ≤ E[|X|p ] p , which means kXk1 ≤ kXkp . This can be extended as kXkr ≤ kXkp if r ≤ p. So it follows that Lp (Ω, A, P) ⊂ Lr (Ω, A, P). For p = q = 2 we get the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality as follows 1 1 E[|XY |] ≤ E[|X|2 ] 2 E[|Y |2 ] 2 . With Y = 1 we have E[|X|]2 = E[X 2 ]. Definition 3.1 (Variance). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Consider a r.v. X ∈ L2 (Ω, A, P). The variance of X is defined as V ar(X) = E[(X − E[X])2 ] and the standard deviation of X is given by p σX = V ar(X). Remark 3.2. Informally, the variance represents the deviation of X around its mean E[X]. Note that V ar(X) = 0 if and only if X is a.s. constant.  <!-- lvl=1 i=64 sid=body[1]/p[65] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Consequently, we get V ar(X) = inf E[(X − a)2 ]. a∈R Proof. V ar(X) = E[(X−E[X])2 ] = E[X 2 −{E[X]X+E[X]2 }] = E[X 2 ]−2E[X]E[X]+E[X]2 = E[X 2 ]−E[X]2 Moreover, we have E[(X − a)2 ] = E[(X − E[X]) + (E[X] − a)2 ] = V ar(X) + (E[X] − a)2 , which implies that for all a ∈ R E[(X − a)2 ] ≥ V ar(X) and there is equality when a = E[X]. Remark 3.4. It follows that if X is centered (i.e. E[X] = 0), we get V ar(X) = E[X 2 ]. More- over, the following two simple inequalities are very often used. (i) ( Markov inequality) If X ≥ 0 and a ≥ 0 then 1 P[X > a] ≤ E[X] . a (ii) ( Tchebisheff inequality) 1 P[|X − E[X]| > a] ≤ V ar(X) . a2 Proof of Remark 3.4. We want to show both inequalities. (i) Note that   X X P[X ≥ a] = E[1{X≥a} ] ≤ E  1{X≥a}  ≤ E .  a | {z } a ≤1 (ii) This follows from (1) because |X − E[X]| is a positive r.v. and hence 1 P[|X − E[X]| ≥ a] = P[|X − E[X]|2 ≥ a2 ] ≤ 2 E[|X − E[X]|2 ] . a | {z } V ar(X) Definition 3.5 (Covariance). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Consider two r.v.’s X, Y ∈ L2 (Ω, A, P). The covariance of X and Y is defined as Cov(X, Y ) = E[(X − E[X])(Y − E[Y ])] = E[XY ] − E[X]E[Y ]. If X = (X1 , ..., Xd ) ∈ Rd is a r.v. such that ∀i ∈ {1, ..., d}, Xi ∈ L2 (Ω, A, P), then the covariance matrix of X is defined as KX = (Cov(Xi , Xj ))1≤i,j≤d . Informally speaking, the covariance between X and Y measures the correlation between X and Y . Note that Cov(X, X) = V ar(X) and from Cauchy-Schwarz we get p p |Cov(X, Y )| ≤ V ar(X) · V ar(Y ). The application (X, Y ) 7→ Cov(X, Y ) is a bilinear form on L2 (Ω, A, P). We also note that KX is symmetric and positive, i.e. if λ1 , ..., λd ∈ R, λ = (λ1 , ..., λd )T , then d X hKX λ, λi = λi λj Cov(Xi , Xj ) ≥ 0. i,j=1  <!-- lvl=1 i=66 sid=body[1]/p[67] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. If A, B ∈ A, we say that A and B are independent if P[A ∩ B] = P[A]P[B]. Example 5.1 (Throw of a die). We have the state space Ω = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}, ω ∈ Ω. Hence we have P[{ω}] = 61 . Now let A = {1, 2} and B = {1, 3, 5}. Then 1 1 1 P[A ∩ B] = P[{1}] = and P[A] = , P[B] = 6 3 2 Therefore we get P[A ∩ B] = P[A]P[B]. Hence we get that A and B are independent.  <!-- lvl=1 i=72 sid=body[1]/p[73] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
- 5. INDEPENDENCE 39 Definition 5.2 (Independence of events). We say that the n events A1 , ..., An ∈ A are inde- pendent if ∀{j1 , ..., jl } ⊂ {1, ..., n} we have P[Aj1 ∩ Aj2 ∩ · · · ∩ Ajl ] = P[Aj1 ] · · · P[Ajl ]. Remark 5.3. It is not enough to have P[A1 ∩ · · · ∩ An ] = P[A1 ] · · · P[An ]. It is also not enough to check that ∀{i, j} ⊂ {1, ..., n}, P[Ai ∩ Aj ] = P[Ai ]P[Aj ]. For instance, let us consider two tosses of a coin and consider events A, B and C given by A = {H at the first throw}, B = {T at the first throw}, C = {same outcome for both tosses} The events A, B and C are two by two independent but A, B and C are not independent events. Proposition 5.4. The n events A1 , ..., An ∈ A are independent if and only if (∗) P[B1 ∩ · · · ∩ Bn ] = P[B1 ] · · · P[Bn ] for all Bi ∈ σ(Ai ) = {∅, Ai , AC i , Ω}, ∀i ∈ {1, ..., n}. Proof. If the above is satisfied and if {j1 , ..., jl } ⊂ {1, ..., n}, then for i ∈ {j1 , ..., jl } take Bi = Ai and for i 6∈ {j1 , ..., jl } take Bi = Ω. So it follows that P[Aj1 ∩ · · · ∩ Ajl ] = P[Aj1 ] · · · P[Ajl ]. Conversely, assume that A1 , ..., An ∈ A are independent and we want to deduce (∗). We can assume that ∀i ∈ {1, ..., n} we have Bi 6= ∅ (for otherwise the identity is trivially satisfied). If {j1 , ..., jl } = {i | Bi 6= Ω}, we have to check that P[Bj1 ∩ · · · ∩ Bjl ] = P[Bj1 ] · · · P[Bjl ], as soon as Bjk = Ajk or Bjk = AC jk . Finally it’s enough to show that if C1 , ..., Cp are independent events, then C1C , C2 , ..., Cp are also independent. But if 1 6∈ {i1 , ..., iq }, for all {i1 , ..., iq } ⊂ {1, ..., p}, then from the definition of independence we have P[Ci1 ∩ · · · ∩ Ciq ] = P[Ci1 ] · · · P[Ciq ]. If 1 ∈ {i1 , ..., iq }, say 1 = i1 , then P[CiC1 ∩ Ci2 ∩ · · · ∩ Ciq ] = P[Ci1 ∩ · · · ∩ Ciq ] − P[C1 ∩ Ci2 ∩ · · · ∩ Ciq ] = P[Ci2 ] · · · P[Ciq ] − P[C1 ]P[Ci2 ] · · · P[Ciq ] = (1 − P[C1 ])P[Ci2 ] · · · P[Ciq ] = P[C1C ]P[Ci2 ] · · · P[Ciq ] Definition 5.5 (Conditional probability). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let A, B ∈ A such that P[B] > 0. The conditional probability of A given B is then defined as P[A ∩ B] P[A | B] = . P[B] Theorem 5.6. Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let A, B ∈ A and suppose that P[B] > 0. (i) A and B are independent if and only if P[A | B] = P[A]. (ii) The map A → [0, 1], A 7→ P[A | B] defines a new probability measure on A called the conditional probability given B. Proof. We need to show both points.  <!-- lvl=1 i=74 sid=body[1]/p[75] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Definition 5.10 (Independence of σ-Algebras). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. We say that the sub σ-Algebras B1 , ..., Bn of A are independent if for all A1 ∈ B1 , ..., An ∈ Bn we get P[A1 ∩ · · · ∩ An ] = P[A1 ] · · · P[An ]. Let now X1 , ..., Xn be n r.v.’s with values in measureable spaces (E1 , E1 ), ..., (En , En ) respec- tively. We say that the r.v.’s X1 , ..., Xn are independent if the σ-Algebras σ(X1 ), ..., σ(Xn ) are independent. This is equivalent to the fact that for all F1 ∈ E1 , ..., Fn ∈ En we have P[{X1 ∈ F1 } ∩ · · · ∩ {Xn ∈ Fn }] = P[X1 ∈ F1 ] · · · P[Xn ∈ Fn ]. (This comes from the fact that for all i ∈ {1, ..., n} we have that σ(Xi ) = {Xi−1 (F ) | F ∈ Ei }) Remark 5.11. If B1 , ..., Bn are n independent sub σ-Algebras and if X1 , ..., Xn are independent r.v.’s such that Xi is Bi measurable for all i ∈ {1, ..., n}, then X1 , ..., Xn are independent r.v.’s (This comes from the fact that for all i ∈ {1, ..., n} we have that σ(Xi ) ⊂ Bi ). Remark 5.12. The n events A1 , ..., An ∈ A are independent if and only if σ(A1 ), ..., σ(An ) are independent. Theorem 5.13 (Independence of Random Variables). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let X1 , ..., Xn be n r.v.’s. Then X1 , ..., Xn are independent if and only if the law of the vector (X1 , ..., Xn ) is the product of the laws of X1 , ..., Xn , i.e. P(X1 ,...,Xn ) = PX1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ PXn . Hence the monotone class theorem implies that P(X1 ,...,Xn ) = PX1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ PXn . R |v| 7. Convergence of Random Variables 7.1. Types of Convergences. We have already seen the notion of a.s. convergence. There are different types of convergences for r.v.’s in probability theory. Let us recall the notion of a.s. convergence. Definition 7.1 (Almost sure convergence). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let (Xn )n≥1 be a sequence of r.v.’s and let X be a r.v. with values in Rd . Then hn oi lim n→∞ Xn = X ⇐⇒ P ω ∈ Ω | lim X n (ω) = X(ω) = 1. n→∞ a.s. Remark 7.2. Another very important convergence type is the Lp -convergence as it is described in measure theory. Recall that convergence in Lp for p ∈ [1, ∞) in the probability language means lim Xn = X ⇐⇒ lim E [|Xn − X|p ] = 0. n→∞ n→∞ Lp Definition 7.3 (Convergence in probability). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. We say that the sequence (Xn )n≥1 converges in probability to X if for all > 0 lim Xn = X ⇐⇒ lim P[|Xn − X| > ] = 0. n→∞ n→∞ P  <!-- lvl=1 i=96 sid=body[1]/p[97] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

The strong law of large numbers. Theorem 7.7 (Kolmogorov’s 0-1 law). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let (Xn )n≥1 be a sequence of independent r.v.’s with values in arbitrary measure spaces. For n ≥ 1, define the σ-Algebra Bn := σ(Xk | k ≥ n). The tail σ-Algebra B∞ is defined as ∞ \ B∞ := Bn . n=1 Then B∞ is trivial in the sense that for all B ∈ B∞ we get that P[B] ∈ {0, 1}. Remark 7.8. We can easily see that a r.v. which is B∞ -measurable is constant a.s. indeed its distribution function can only take the values 0 and 1. Proof. Define Dn := σ(Xk | k ≤ n). We have already observed that Dn and Bn+1 are independent and hence since B∞ ⊂ Bn+1S , we get that for all n ≥ 1, Dn and B∞ are also independent. This implies that for all A ∈ ∞n=1 Dn and for all B ∈ B∞ we get P[A ∩ B] = P[A]P[B]. S∞ S∞ Since n=1 Dn is stable under finite intersection, we obtain that σ ( n=1 Dn ) is independent of B∞ and ∞ ! [ σ Dn = σ (X1 , X2 , ...) . n=1 We also note that the fact that B∞ ⊂ σ(X1 , X2 , ...) implies that B∞ is independent of itself. Thus it follows that for all B ∈ B∞ , we get P[B] = P[B ∩ B] = P[B]P[B] = P[B]2 . Hence P[B] = P[B]2 and therefore P[B] ∈ {0, 1}. Remark 7.9. Theorem 7.11 (Strong law of large numbers). Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. Let (Xn )n≥1 be a sequence of iid r.v.’s, such that Xi ∈ L1 (Ω, A, P) for all i ∈ {1, ..., n}. The assumption E[X1 ] < ∞ is important, but if X1 ≥ 0 and E[X1 ] = ∞ we can apply the theorem to X1 ∧ k for k > 0, and obtain that the theorem also holds with E[X1 ] = ∞. Proof. Let Sn = X1 + ... + Xn with S0 = 0 and take a > E[X1 ]. Define M = supn>0 (Sn − na). We shall show that M < ∞ a.s. Since we obviously have Sn ≤ na + M , it follows immediately that Snn ≤ a a.s. So it follows that Sn Sn lim inf = lim sup = E[X1 ] a.s. n n n n Hence we only need to show that M < ∞ a.s. We first note that {M < ∞} ∈ B∞ . Indeed, for all k ≥ 0 we get that {M < ∞} = {supn∈N (Sn −an) < ∞} = {supn≥k (Sn −Sk −(n−k)a) < ∞}. So it follows that P[M < ∞] ∈ {0, 1}. Now we need to show that P[M < ∞] = 1 or equivalently P[M = ∞] < 1. We do it by contradiction. For k ∈ N, set Mk = sup0≤n≤k (Sn − na) and Mk0 = sup0≤n≤k (Sn+1 −Sn −na). Then Mk and Mk0 have the same distribution. Indeed, (X1 , ..., Xk ) and (X2 , ..., Xk+1 ) have the same distribution and Mk = Fk (X1 , ..., Xk ) and Mk0 = Fk (X2 , ..., Xk+1 ) with some map Fk : Rk → R. Moreover, M = limk→∞ ↑ Mk and therefore M 0 = limk→∞ Mk . Since Mk and Mk0 have the same distribution, M and M 0 also have the same distribution. Indeed P[M 0 ≤ X] = limk→∞ ↓ P[Mk0 ≤ X] = limk→∞ ↓ P[Mk ≤ X] = P[M ≤ X]. So M and M 0 have the same distribution function. Moreover, Mk+1 = sup{0, sup1≤n≤k+1 (Sn − na)} = sup{0, Mk0 + X1 − a}, which implies that Mk+1 = Mk0 − inf{a − X1 , Mk0 }. Now we can use the fact that Mk0 and Mk are bounded to obtain E[inf{a − X1 , Mk0 }] = E[Mk0 ] − E[Mk+1 ] ≤ |a − X1 | ≤ |a| + |X1 | ∈ L1 (Ω, A, P) and apply the dominated convergence theorem to obtain E[inf{a − X1 , M 0 }] ≤ 0. 1Hint: Borel-Cantelli  <!-- lvl=1 i=102 sid=body[1]/p[103] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Before law law stating and proving the central limit theorem, we give two extra results on convergence in law. Theorem 9.12. Let (Ω, A, P) be a probability space. - 74 3. CONDITIONAL EXPECTATIONS We want to give here more, maybe a bit harder, examples of Hilbert spaces used in functional analysis. (i) Sobolev spaces: Sobolev spaces, denoted by H s or W s,2 , are Hilbert spaces. These are a special kind of a function space in which differentiation may be performed, but that support the structure of an inner product. Because differentiation is permitted, Sobolev spaces are a convenient setting for the theory of partial differential equations. They also form the basis of the theory of direct methods in the calculus of variations. For s a nonnegative integer and Ω ⊂ Rn , the Sobolev space H s (Ω) contains L2 -functions whose weak derivatives of order up to s are also in L2 . The inner product in H s (Ω) is Z Z Z hf, giH s (Ω) = f (x)ḡ(x)dµ(x) + Df (x) · Dḡ(x)dµ(x) + ... + Ds f (x) · Ds ḡ(x)dµ(x), Ω Ω Ω where the dot indicates the dot product in the euclidean space of partial derivatives of each order. Slobber spaces can also be defined when s is not an integer. (ii) Hardy spaces: The Hardy spaces are function spaces, arising in complex analysis and harmonic analysis, whose elements are certain holomorphic functions in a complex domain. Let U denote the unit disc in the complex plane. Then the Hardy space H2 (U ) is defined as the space of holomorphic functions f on U such that the means Z 2π 1 Mr (f ) = |f (reiθ )|2 dθ 2π 0 remains bounded for r < 1. The norm on this Hardy space is defined by p kf kH2 (U ) = lim Mr (f ). r→1 Hardy spaces P∞in the ndisc are related P∞ to Fourier series. A function f is in H2 (U ) if and only if f (z) = n=0 an z , where n=0 |an | < ∞. Thus H2 (U ) consists of those functions that 2 are L2 on the circle and whose negative frequency Fourier coefficients vanish. (iii) Bergman spaces: The Bergman spaces are another family of Hilbert spaces of holomor- phic functions. Let D be a bounded open set in the complex plane (or a higher-dimensional complex space) and let L2,h (D) be the space of holomorphic functions f in D that are also in L2 (D) in the sense that Z kf k2 = |f (z)|2 dµ(z) < ∞, D where the integral is taken with respect to the Lebesgue measure in D. Clearly L2,h (D) is a subspace of L2 (D); in fact, it is a closed subspace and so a Hilbert space in its own right. This is a consequence of the estimate, valid on compact subsets K of D, that sup |f (z)| ≤ CK kf k2 , z∈K which in turn follows from Cauchy’s integral formula. Thus convergence of a sequence of holomorphic functions in L2 (D) implies also compact convergence and so the function is also holomorphic. Another consequence of this inequality is that the linear functional that evaluates a function f at a point of D is actually continuous on L2,h (D). The Riesz representation theorem (see notes on measure and integral) implies that the evaluation functional can be represented as an element of L2,h (D). Thus, for every z ∈ D, there is a function ηz ∈ L2,h (D) such that Z f (z) = f (ζ)ηz (ζ)dµ(ζ) D for all f ∈ L2,h (D). The integrand K(ζ, z) = ηz (ζ) is known as the Bergman kernel of D. This integral kernel satisfies a reproducing property Z f (z) = f (ζ)K(ζ, z)dµ(ζ). D  <!-- lvl=1 i=142 sid=body[1]/p[143] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
- 1. L2 (Ω, F , P) AS A HILBERT SPACE AND ORTHOGONAL PROJECTIONS 75 A Bergman space is an example of a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, which is a Hilbert space of functions along with a kernel K(ζ, z) that verifies a reproducing property analogous to this one. The Hardy space H2 (D) also admits a reproducing kernel, known as the Szegö kernel. Reproducing kernels are common in other areas of mathematics as well. For instance, in harmonic analysis the Poisson kernel is a reproducing kernel for the Hilbert space of square integrable harmonic functions in the unit ball. That the latter is a Hilbert space at all is a consequence of the mean value theorem for harmonic functions. Remark 1.5. The notion of Hilbert spaces allow us to do basic geometry on them. Even if our space is an infinite dimensional vector space, we can still make sense of geometrical meanings, for example orthogonality, only by using the inner product on the space. Definition 1.6 (Orthogonal). Two elements X, Y in a Hilbert space (H, h·, ·i) are said to be orthogonal if hX, Y i = 0. Remark 1.7. For a real valued Hilbert space H we get the following identity. For every X, Y ∈ H kX + Y k2 = hX + Y, X + Y i = hX, Xi + hX, Y i + hY, Xi + hY, Y i = kXk2 + kY k2 + 2hX, Y i, and if X and Y are orthogonal, i.e. hX, Y i = 0, we get the usual pythagorean relation kX + Y k2 = kXk2 + kY k2 . Theorem 1.8. Let (Xn )n≥1 and (Yn )n≥1 be two converging sequences in a Hilbert space H such n→∞ n→∞ that Xn −−−→ X and Yn −−−→ Y . Then n→∞ hXn , Yn i −−−→ hX, Y i. n→∞ (In particular, Xn = Yn gives us that kXn k −−−→ kXk) Proof. We can look at the difference, which is given by |hX, Y i − hXn , Yn i| = |hX − Xn , Y i + hXn , Y i − hXn , Yn i| = |hX − Xn , Y i + hXn , Y − Yn i| ≤ |hX − Xn , Y i| + |Xn , Y − Yn i| ≤ kX − Xn kkY k + kXn kkY − Yn k, where we have used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Now since for n large enough there is some ε > 0 such that kX − Xn k < ε and kY − Yn k < ε by assumption, and the fact that kXn k is bounded independently of n, we get the claim. Lemma 1.9 (Parallelogram identity). Let H be a Hilbert space. For all X, Y ∈ H we get kX + Y k2 + kX − Y k2 = 2(kXk2 + kY k2 ). Moreover if a norm satisfies the parallelogram identity, it can be derived from an inner product. Proof. Exercise1. Definition 1.10. Closed linear subset Let H be a Hilbert space and let L ⊂ H be a linear n→∞ subset. L is called closed if for every sequence (Xn )n≥1 in L with Xn −−−→ X we get that X ∈ L. Theorem 1.11. Let H be a Hilbert space and let Γ ⊂ H be a subset. Let Γ⊥ denote the set of all elements of H which are orthogonal to Γ, i.e. Γ⊥ = {X ∈ H | hX, γi = 0, ∀γ ∈ Γ}. Then Γ⊥ is a closed subspace of H. We call Γ⊥ the orthogonal complement of Γ. 1Use the fact kX + Y k2 = hX + Y, X + Y i. This proof can be found in the notes of measure and integral  <!-- lvl=1 i=144 sid=body[1]/p[145] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
- 76 3. CONDITIONAL EXPECTATIONS Proof. Let α, β ∈ R and X, X 0 ∈ Γ⊥ . It is clear that for all Y ∈ Γ hαX + βX 0 , Y i = 0. Hence Γ⊥ is a linear subspace of H. Next we want to check whether it’s closed. Take a sequence n→∞ (Xn )n≥1 in Γ⊥ such that Xn −−−→ X with X ∈ H. Now for all Y ∈ Γ we get hXn , Y i = 0 n→∞ and hXn , Y i −−−→ hX, Y i because of the previous theorem. Hence hX, Y i = 0 and therefore X ∈ Γ⊥ and the claim follows. Definition 1.12 (Distance to a closed subspace). Let H be a Hilbert space and let X ∈ H. Moreover let L ⊂ H be a closed subspace. The distance of X to L is given by d(X, L) = inf kX − Y k = inf{kX − Y k | Y ∈ L}. Y ∈L Remark 1.13. Since L is closed, X ∈ L if and only if d(X, L) = 0. 1.1. Convex sets in uniformly Convex spaces. While the emphasis in this section is on Hilbert spaces, it is useful to isolate a more abstract property which is precisely what is needed for several proofs. Definition 1.14 (Uniformly convex vector space). A normed vector space (V, k · k) is called uniformly convex if for X, Y ∈ V X +Y kXk, kY k ≤ 1 =⇒ ≤ 1 − ψ(kX − Y k), 2 where ψ : [0, 2] → [0, 1] is a monotonically increasing function with ψ(r) > 0 for all r > 0. Lemma 1.15. A Hilbert space (H, h·, ·i) is uniformly convex. Proof. For X, Y ∈ H with kXk, kY k ≤ 1 then by parallelogram identity we have r r X +Y | 1 1 1 = 2kXk + kY k − kX − Y k ≤ 1 − kX − Y k2 = 1 − ψ(kX − Y k) 2 2 2 2 1 2 2 2 q as required, with ψ(r) = 1 − 1 − 12 r2 . Heuristically, we can think of Definition 1.4 as having the following geometrical meaning. If vectors X and Y have norm (length) one, then their mid-point X+Y 2 has much smaller norm unless X and Y are very close together. This accords closely with the geometrical intuition from finite-dimensions spaces with euclidean distance. The following theorem, whose conclusion is illustrated in the figure, will have many important consequences for the study of Hilbert spaces. Theorem 1.16 (Unique approximation of a closed convex set). Let (V, k · k) be a Banach space with a uniformly convex norm, let K ⊂ V be a closed convex subset and assume that v0 ∈ V . Then there exists a unique element w ∈ K that is closest to v0 in the sense that w is the only element of K with kw − v0 k = d(v0 , K) = inf kk − v0 k. k∈K Proof. By translating both the set K and the point v0 by −v0 we may assume without loss of generality that v0 = 0. We define s = inf kk − v0 k = inf kkk. k∈K k∈K If s = 0, then we must have 0 ∈ K since K is closed and the only choice is then w = v0 = 0 (the uniqueness of w is a consequence of the strict positivity of the norm). So assume that s > 0. By multiplying by the scalar 1s we have found a point w ∈ K with norm 1, then its uniqueness is an immediate consequence of the uniform convexity: if w1 , w2 ∈ K have kw1 k = kw2 k = 1, then w1 +w2 2 ∈ K because K is convex. Also, w1 +w 2 2 = 1 by the triangle inequality and since s = 1. By uniform convexity this implies that w1 = w2 . Turning to the existence, let us first sketch the argument. Choose a sequence (kn ) in K with kkn k → 1 as n → ∞. Then the mid-points kn +km 2 also lie in K, since K is convex. However, this shows that the mid-point must have norm  <!-- lvl=1 i=146 sid=body[1]/p[147] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Therefore kn and km must be close together by uniform convexity. Making this precise, we will see that (kn ) is a Cauchy sequence. Since V is complete and K is closed, this will give a point w ∈ K with kwk = 1 = s as required. To make this more precise, we apply uniform convexity to the normalized vectors 1 xn = kn , sn where sn = kkn k. The mid-point of xn and xm can now be expressed as xm + xn 1 1 1 1 = km + kn = + akm + bkn ) 2 2sm 2sn 2sm 2sn with 1 2sm a= 1 1 ≥0 2sm + 2sn 1 2sn b= 1 1 ≥0 2sm + 2sn and a + b = 1. Therefore akm + bkn ∈ K by convexity and so xm + xn 1 1 1 1 = + kakm + bkn k ≥ + . 2 2sm 2sn 2sm 2sn Let ψ be as in Definition 1.4 and fix ε > 0. Choose N = N (ε) large enough to ensure that m ≥ N implies that 1 ≥ 1 − ψ(ε). sm Then m, n ≥ N implies that 1 1 + ≥ 1 − ψ(ε), 2sm sn which together with the definition of uniform convexity gives xm + xn 1 − ψ(kxm − xn k) ≥ ≥ 1 − ψ(ε). 2 By monotonicity of the function ψ this implies for all m, n ≥ N that kxm − xn k ≤ ε, showing that (xn ) is a Cauchy sequence. As V is assumed to be complete, we deduce that (xn ) converges to some x ∈ V . Since sn → 1 and kn → sn xn as n → ∞ it follows that limn→∞ kn = x. As K is closed the limit x belongs to K and by contradiction is an (and hence is the unique) element closest to v0 = 0. Remark 1.17. This unique approximation is clearly true for Hilbert spaces, since they are uni- formly convex spaces. Corollary 1.18 (Orthogonal decomposition). Let H be a Hilbert space and let L ⊂ H be a closed subspace. Discrete construction of the conditional expectation. Let X and Y be two r.v.’s on a probability space (Ω, F, P). Let Y take values in R and X take values in a countable discrete set {x1 , x2 , ..., xn , ...}. The goal is to describe the expectation of the r.v. Y by knowing the observed r.v. X. For instance, let X = xj ∈ {x1 , x2 , ..., xn , ...}. Therefore we look at a set {ω ∈ Ω | X(ω) = xj } rather than looking at whole Ω. For Λ ∈ F, we thus define Q[Λ] = P[Λ | {X = xj }], a new probability measure Q, with P[X = xj ] > 0. Therefore it makes more sense to compute Z Z EQ [Y ] = Y (ω)dQ(ω) = Y (ω)dP(ω) Ω {ω∈Ω|X(ω)=xj } rather than Z Z EP [Y ] = Y (ω)dP(ω) = ydPY (y). Ω R Definition 2.2 (Conditional expectation (X discrete, Y real valued, single value case)). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X : Ω → {x1 , x2 , ..., xn , ...} be a r.v. taking values in a discrete set and let Y be a real valued r.v. on that space. If P[X = xj ] > 0, we can define the conditional expectation of Y given {X = xj } to be E[Y | X = xj ] = EQ [Y ], where Q is the probability measure on F defined by Q[Λ] = P[Λ | X = xj ], for Λ ∈ F, provided that EQ [|Y |] < ∞. Theorem 2.3 (Conditional expectation (X discrete, Y discrete, single value case)). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X be a r.v. on that space with values in {x1 , x2 , ..., xn , ...} and let Y also be a r.v. with values in {y1 , y2 , ..., yn , ...}. If P[X = xj ] > 0, we can write the conditional expectation of Y given {X = xj } as ∞ X E[Y | X = xj ] = yk P[Y = yk | X = xj ]. k=1 provided that the series is absolutely convergent. 3Use the previous facts for the proof of Baye’s formula. Definition 2.5 (Conditional expectation (X discrete, Y real valued, complete case)). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X be a countably valued r.v. and let Y be a real valued r.v. The conditional expectation of Y given X is defined by E[Y | X] = f (X), with f as in (2), provided that for all j: if Qj [Λ] = P[Λ | X = xj ], with P[X = xj ] > 0, we get EQj [|Y |] < ∞. Remark 2.6. The above definition does not define E[Y | X] everywhere but rather almost ev- erywhere, since on each set {X = x}, where P[X = x] = 0, its value is arbitrary. Example 2.7. Let5 X ∼ Π(λ). Let us consider a tossing game, where we say that when X = n, we do n independent tossing of a coin where each time one obtains 1 with probability p ∈ [0, 1] and 0 with probability 1 − p. Define also S to be the r.v. giving the total number of 1 obtained in the game. Therefore, if X = n is given, we get that S is binomial distributed with parameters (p, n). We want to compute (i) E[S | X] (ii) E[X | S] Remark 2.8. It is more natural to ask for the expectation of the amount of 1 obtained for the whole game by knowing how many games were played. The reverse is a bit more difficult. Logically, we may also notice that it definitely doesn’t make sense to say S ≥ X, because we can not obtain more wins in a game than the amount of games that were played. (i) First we compute E[S | X = n]: If X = n, we know that S is binomial distributed with parameters (p, n) (S ∼ B(p, n)) and therefore we already know6 E[S | X = n] = pn. Now we need to identify the function f defined as in (2) by f : N −→ R n 7−→ pn. Therefore we get by definition E[S | X] = pX. (ii) Next we want to compute E[X | S = k]: For n ≥ k we have n k n−k e−λ λn P[S = k | X = n]P[X = n] k p (1 − p) n! P[X = n | S = k] = = P∞ m k m , e−λ λm! Continuous construction of the conditional expectation. Now we want to define E[Y | X], where X is no longer assumed to be countably valued. Therefore we want to recall the following two facts: Definition 2.9 (σ-Algebra generated by a random variable). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X : (Ω, F, P) → (Rn , B(Rn ), λ) be a r.v. on that space. The σ-Algebra generated by X is given by σ(X) = X −1 (B(Rn )) = {A ∈ Ω | A = X −1 (B), B ∈ B(Rn )}. Theorem 2.10. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X : (Ω, F, P) → (Rn , B(Rn ), λ) be a r.v. on that space and let Y be a real valued r.v. on that space. Y is measurable with respect to σ(X) if and only if there exists a Borel measurable function f : Rn → R such that Y = f (X). Remark 2.11. We want to make use of the fact that for the Hilbert space L2 (Ω, F, P) we get that L2 (Ω, σ(X), P) ⊂ L2 (Ω, F, P) is a complete subspace, since σ(X) ⊂ F. This allows us to use the orthogonal projections and to interpret the conditional expectation as such a projection. Definition 2.12 (Conditional expectation (as a projection onto a closed subspace)). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Y ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P). Then the conditional expectation of Y given X is the unique element Ŷ ∈ L2 (Ω, σ(X), P) such that for all Z ∈ L2 (Ω, σ(X), P) (2.2) E[Y Z] = E[Ŷ Z]. This result is due to the fact that if Y − Ŷ ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P) then for all Z ∈ L2 (Ω, σ(X), P) we get hY − Ŷ , Zi = 0. We write E[Y | X] for Ŷ . Remark 2.13. Ŷ is the orthogonal projection of Y onto L2 (Ω, σ(X), P). Remark 2.14. Since X takes values in Rn , there exists a Borel measurable function f : Rn → R such that E[Y | X] = f (X) with E[f (X)] < ∞. We can also rewrite (3) as: for all Borel measurable g : Rn → R, such that 2 E[g 2 (X)] < ∞, we get E[Y g(X)] = E[f (X)g(X)]. Now let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F and consider the space L2 (Ω, G, P) ⊂ L2 (Ω, F, P). It is clear that L2 (Ω, G, P) is a Hilbert space and thus we can project to it. Definition 2.15 (Conditional expectation (projection case)). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Y ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P) and let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F. Then the conditional expectation of Y given G is defined as the unique element E[Y | G] ∈ L2 (Ω, G, P) such that for all Z ∈ L2 (Ω, G, P) (2.3) E[Y Z] = E[E[Y | G]Z]. Remark 2.16. In (3) or (1), it is enough7 to restrict the test r.v. Z to the class of r.v.’s of the form Z = 1A , A ∈ G. 7Since we can always consider linear combinations of 1 A and then apply density theorems to it  <!-- lvl=1 i=158 sid=body[1]/p[159] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

So in particular, any statement like E[Y | G] ≥ 0 or E[Y | G] = Z has to be understood with an implicit a.s. Theorem 2.18. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Y ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P) and let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F. (i) If Y ≥ 0, then E[Y | G] ≥ 0 (ii) E[E[Y | G]] = E[Y ] (iii) The map Y 7→ E[Y | G] is linear. Proof. For (i) take Z = 1{E[Y |G]<0} to obtain E[Y Z] = E[E[Y | G]Z] . | {z } | {z } ≥0 ≤0 This implies that P[E[Y | G] < 0] = 0. For (ii) take Z = 1Ω and plug into (4). For (iii) notice that linearity comes from the orthogonal projection operator. But we can also do it directly by taking Y, Y 0 ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P), α, β ∈ R and Z ∈ L2 (Ω, G, P) to obtain E[(αY +βY 0 )Z] = E[Y Z]+βE[Y 0 Z] = αE[E[Y | G]Z]+βE[E[Y 0 | G]Z] = E[(αE[Y | G]+βE[Y 0 | G])Z]. Now we can conclude by using the uniqueness property that E[αY + βY 0 | G] = αE[Y | G] + βE[Y 0 | G]. Now we want to extend the definition of the conditional expectation to r.v.’s in L1 (Ω, F, P) or to L+ (Ω, F, P), which is the space of non negative r.v.’s allowing the value ∞. Lemma 2.19. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Y ∈ L+ (Ω, F, P) and let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F. Then there exists a unique element E[Y | G] ∈ L+ (Ω, G, P) such that for all X ∈ L+ (Ω, G, P) (2.4) E[Y X] = E[E[Y | G]X] and this conditional expectation agrees with the previous definition when Y ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P). More- over, if 0 ≤ Y ≤ Y 0 , then E[Y | G] ≤ E[Y 0 | G]. Proof. If Y ≤ 0 and Y ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P), then we define E[Y | G] as before. Using the monotone convergence theorem we get E[Y X] = E[Y lim Xn ] = lim E[Y Xn ] = lim E[E[Y | G]Xn ] = E[E[Y | G] lim X] = E[E[Y | G]X]. n→∞ n→∞ n→∞ n→∞ This shows that (5) is true whenever Y ∈ L (Ω, F, P) with Y ≥ 0 and X ∈ L+ (Ω, G, P). Now let 2 Y ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P). Define Ym = Y ∧ m. Hence we get Ym ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P) and Ym ↑ Y as n → ∞. Each E[Ym | G] is well defined8 and positive and increasing. We define E[Y | G] = lim E[Ym | G]. n→∞ Several applications of the monotone convergence theorem will give us for X ∈ L+ (Ω, G, P) E[Y X] = lim E[Ym X] = lim E[E[Ym | G]X] = E[E[Y | G]X]. m→∞ m→∞ Furthermore if 0 ≤ Y ≤ Y , then Y ∧ m ≤ Y 0 ∧ m and therefore 0 E[Y | G] ≤ E[Y 0 | G]. Moreover, {U < V } = n≥1 Λn and therefore P[U < V ] = 0 and similarly P[V < U ] = 0. This implies P[U = V ] = 1. Theorem 2.20. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Y ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) and let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F. Then there exists a unique element E[Y | G] ∈ L1 (Ω, G, P) such that for every X bounded and G-measurable (2.5) E[Y X] = E[E[Y | G]X]. This conditional expectation agrees with the definition for the L2 . Moreover it satisfies: (i) If Y ≥ 0, then E[Y | G] ≥ 0 (ii) The map Y 7→ E[Y | G] is linear. Proof. We will only prove the existence, since the rest is exactly the same as before. Write Y = Y + − Y − with Y + , Y − ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) and Y + , Y − ≥ 0. So E[Y + | G] and E[Y − | G] are well defined. Now we set E[Y | G] = E[Y + | G] − E[Y − | G]. This is well defined because E[E[Y ± | G]] = E[Y ± ] < ∞ if we let X = 1Ω in the previous lemma and therefore E[Y + | G] and E[Y − | G] ∈ L1 (Ω, G, P). For all X bounded and G-measurable we can also write X = X + − X − and it follows from the previous lemma that E[E[Y ± | G]X] = E[Y ± X]. This implies that E[Y | G] satisfies (6). Corollary 2.21. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) be a r.v. on that space. Then E[E[X | G]] = E[X]. Proof. Take equation (4) and set Z = 1Ω . Corollary 2.22. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) be a r.v. on that space. Then |E[X | G]| ≤ E[|X| | G]. In particular E[|E[X | G]|] ≤ E[|X|]. Proof. We can always write X = X + − X − and also |X| = X + + X − . Therefore we get |E[X | G]| = |E[X + | G] − E[X − | G]| ≤ E[X + | G] + E[X − | G] = E[X + + X − | G] = E[|X| | G]. Proposition 2.23. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Y ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) be a r.v. on that space and assume that Y is independent of the sub σ-Algebra G ⊂ F, i.e. σ(Y ) is independent of G. Then E[Y | G] = E[Y ].  <!-- lvl=1 i=162 sid=body[1]/p[163] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

This implies that, since E[Y ] is constant, that E[Y ] ∈ L1 (Ω, G, P) and satisfies (4). Therefore by uniqueness we get that E[Y | G] = E[Y ]. Theorem 2.24. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X and Y be two r.v.’s on that space and let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F. Assume further that at least one of these two holds: (i) X, Y and XY are in L1 (Ω, F, P) with X being G-measurable. (ii) X ≥ 0, Y ≥ 0 with X being G-mearuable. Then E[XY | G] = E[Y | G]X. In particular, if X is a positive r.v. or in L1 (Ω, G, P) and G-measurable, then E[X | G] = X. Proof. For (ii) assume first that X, Y ≤ 0. Let Z be a positive and G-measurable r.v. Then we can obtain E[(XY )Z] = E[Y (XZ)] = E[E[Y | G]XZ] = E[(E[Y | G]X)Z]. Note that E[(E[Y | G]X)Z] is a positive r.v. and G-measurable. Hence E[XY | G] = XE[Y | G]. For (i) we can write X = X + + X − and use (ii). This is an easy exercise. Remark 2.25. Next we want to show that the classical limit theorems from measure theory also make sense in terms of the conditional expectation10. Theorem 2.26 (Limit theorems for the conditional expectation). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let (Yn )n≥1 be a sequence of r.v.’s on that space and let G ⊂ F be a sub σ-Algebra of F. Then we have: (i) ( Monotone convergence) Assume that (Yn )n≥1 is a sequence of positive r.v.’s for all n such that limn→∞ ↑ Yn = Y a.s. Then lim E[Yn | G] = E[Y | G]. n→∞ (ii) ( Fatou) Assume that (Yn )n≥1 is a sequence of positive r.v.’s for all n. Then E[lim inf Yn | G] = lim inf E[Yn | G]. n n n→∞ (iii) ( Dominated convergence) Assume that Yn −−−→ Y a.s. and that there exists Z ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) such that |Yn | ≤ Z for all n. Then lim E[Yn | G] = E[Y | G]. n→∞ Proof. We will only prove (i), since (ii) and (iii) are proved in a similar way (it’s a good exercise to do the proof). Since (Yn )n≥1 is an increasing sequence, it follows that E[Yn+1 | G] ≥ E[Yn | G]. 10Recall the classical limit theorems for integrals: M onotone convergence : Let (f ) R n n≥1 be an increasing se- quence of positive and measurable functions and let f = limn→∞ ↑Rfn . Then f dµ = limn→∞ R fn dµ. F atou : Let (fn )n≥1 be a sequence of measurable and positive functions. Then lim inf n fn dµ ≤ lim inf n fn dµ. Dominated convergence : Let (fn )n≥1 be a Rsequence Rof integrable functions with |fn | ≤ g for all n with g integrable. Denote f = limn→∞ fn . Then limn→∞ fn dµ = f dµ  <!-- lvl=1 i=164 sid=body[1]/p[165] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Let X ∈ 1 (Ω, F, P) such that ϕ(X) ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P). Then ϕ(E[X | G]) ≤ E[ϕ(X) | G] for all sub σ-Algebras G ⊂ F. Proof. Exercise. Example 2.28. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let ϕ(X) = X 2 and let X ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P). Then (E[X | G])2 ≤ E[X 2 | G] for all sub σ-Algebras G ⊂ F. Theorem 2.29 (Tower property). Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) be a positive r.v. on that space. Let C ⊂ G ⊂ F be a tower of sub σ-Algebras of F. Then E[E[X | G]C] = E[X | C]. Proof. Let Z be a bounded and C-measurable r.v. Then we obtain E[XZ] = E[E[X | C]Z]. But Z is also G-measurable and hence we get E[XZ] = E[E[X | G]Z]. Therefore, for all Z bounded and C-measurable r.v.’s, we get E[E[X | G]Z] = E[E[X | C]Z] and thus E[E[X | G]C] = E[X | C]. 3. The Radon-Nikodym approach for the conditional expectation Remark 3.1. Before stating the Radon-Nikodym theorem, we recall some definitions from mea- sure theory. Let (Ω, B) be a measurable space. A measure ν is absolutely continuous with respect to another measure µ, written ν µ if there exists some measurable f ≥ 0 with dν = f dµ, that is if there is a finite measurable f ≥ 0 with Z ν(B) = f dµ B for all B ∈ B. Two measures µ and ν are singular with respect to each other if there exists disjoint measurable sets A1 , A2 ⊂ Ω with Ω = A1 t A2 and with ν(A1 ) = 0 = µ(A2 ). Finally, recall that a measure µ is σ-finite if there is a decomposition of Ω into measurable sets,  <!-- lvl=1 i=166 sid=body[1]/p[167] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
- 3. THE RADON-NIKODYM APPROACH FOR THE CONDITIONAL EXPECTATION 87 ∞ G Ω= Ai i=1 with µ(Ai ) < ∞. Theorem 3.2 (Radon-Nikodym). Let µ and ν be two σ-finite measures on a measurable space (Ω, B). Then ν can be decomposed as ν = νabs + νsing into the sum of two σ-finite measure with νabs µ being absolutely continuous with respect to µ, and with νsing and µ being singular to each other (which will be written νsing ⊥ µ). Remark 3.3. The theorem implies that there exists another, more practical way of checking whether a given σ-finite measure ν is absolutely continuous with respect to another σ-finite measure µ. If µ(N ) = 0 implies that ν(N ) = 0 for every measurable N ⊂ Ω, then ν = νabs is absolutely continuous. We also note that the density function f with f dµ = dν is called the dν Radon-N ikodym derivative and is often written f = dµ . Remark 3.4. To prove this theorem, we need a theorem which gives us a nice relationship between a Hilbert space and its dual space. Actually we can identify a Hilbert space H with its dual space H∗ . Lemma 3.5 (Riesz-representation for Hilbert spaces). For a Hilbert space H, the map sending h ∈ H to φ(h) ∈ H∗ defined by φ(h)(x) = hx, hi is a linear (resp. sesqui-linear in the complex case) isometric isomorphism between H and its dual space H∗ . More properties of the conditional expectation Theorem 4.1. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let G1 ⊂ F and G2 ⊂ F be two sub σ-Algebras of F. Then G1 and G2 are independent if and only if for every positive and G2 -measurable r.v. X (or for X ∈ L1 (Ω, G2 , P) or X = 1A for A ∈ G2 ) we have E[X | G2 ] = E[X]. Proof. We only need to prove that the statement in the bracket implies that G1 and G2 are independent. Assume that for all A ∈ G2 we have that E[1A | G1 ] = P[A] and moreover for all B ∈ G1 we have that E[1B 1A ] = E[E[1A | G1 ]1B ]. Note that E[1A | G1 ] = P[A] and therefore E[1B 1A ] = P[A ∩ B] = P[A]E[1B ] = P[A]P[B] and hence the claim follows. Remark 4.2. Let Z and Y be two real valued r.v.’s. Then Z and Y are independent if and only if for all h Borel measurable, such that E[|h(Z)|] < ∞, we get E[h(Z) | Y ] = E[h(Z)]. To see this we can apply the theorem with G2 = σ(Z) and note that all r.v.’s in L1 (Ω, G2 , P) are of the form h(Z) with E[|h(Z)|] < ∞. In particular, if Z ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P), we get E[Z | Y ] = E[Z]. Be aware that the latter equation does not imply that Y and Z are independent. For example take Z ∼ N (0, 1) and Y = |Z|. Now for all h with E[|h(|Z|)|] < ∞ we get E[Zh(|Z|)] = 0. Thus E[Z | |Z|] = 0, but Z and |Z| are not independent. Theorem 4.3. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X and Y be two r.v.’s on that space with values in the same measure space E and F . In the literature, one abusively note Z E[h(X) | Y = y] = h(x)ν(y, dx), Rm and ν(y, dx) is called the conditional distribution of X given Y = y (even though in general we have P[Y = y] = 0). 4.1.2. The Gaussian case. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X, Y1 , ..., Yp ∈ L2 (Ω, F, P). We saw that E[X | Y1 , ..., Yp ] is the orthogonal projection of X on L2 (Ω, σ(Y1 , ..., Yp ), P). Since this conditional expectation is σ(Y1 , ..., Yp )-measurable, it is of the form ϕ(Y1 , ..., Yp ). In gen- eral, L2 (Ω, σ(Y1 , ..., Yp ), P) is of infinite dimension, so it is bad to obtain ϕ explicitly. We also saw that ϕ(Y1 , ..., Yp ) is the best approximation of X in the L2 (Ω, σ(Y1 , ..., Yp ), P) sense by an element of L2 (Ω, σ(Y1 , ..., Yp ), P). Moreover, it is well known that the best L2 -approximation of X by an affine function of 1, Y1 , ..., Yp is the best orthogonal projection of X on the vector space {1, Y1 , ..., Yp }, i.e. E[(X − (α0 + α1 Y1 + · · · + αp Yp )2 )].  <!-- lvl=1 i=174 sid=body[1]/p[175] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Basic facts on Gaussian vectors A random vector Z = (Z1 , ..., Zn ) is said to be Gaussian, if for all λ1 , ..., λn ∈ R λ1 Z1 + · · · + λn Zn is Gaussian.Moreover, Z is called centered, if E[Zj ] = 0 for all 1 ≤ j ≤ n. Let Z be a Gaussian vector. Then for all ξ ∈ Rn we get h ihξ,Zi i 1 t E e = exp − ξ CZ ξ , 2 where CZ := (Cij ) and Cij = E[Zi Zj ]. If Cov(Zi , Zj ) = 0, then Zi and Zj are independent. More generally, we have the Gaussian vectors (X1 , ..., Xi1 , Xi1 +1 , ..., Xi2 , ..., Xin−1 +1 , ..., Xin ). | {z } | {z } | {z } Y1 Y2 Yn Y1 and Y2 are independent if and only if Cov(Xj , Xn ) = 0, where 1 ≤ j ≤ i1 and i1 + 1 ≤ k ≤ i2 . If Z1 , ..., Zn are independent Gaussian r.v.’s, we have that Z = (Z1 , ..., Zn ) is a Gaussian vector. If Z is a Gaussian vector and A ∈ M(m × n, R) , we get that AZ is again a Gaussian vector. Theorem 5.1. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let X ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) and Y1 , ..., Yp ∈ L1 (Ω, F, P) and let (X, Y1 , ..., Yp ) be a centered Gaussian vector. Then E[X | Y1 , ..., Yp ] is the orthogonal projection of X on the vector space span{Y1 , ..., Yp }. Consequently, there exists real numbers λ1 , ..., λp such that E[X | Y1 , ..., Yp ] = λ1 Y1 + · · · + λp Yp . Remark 5.2. Moreover, for a measurable map h : R → R+ we get Z E[h(X) | Y1 , ..., Yp ] = h(x)QPnj=1 λj Yj ,σ2 (x)dx, h i R where σ 2 = E X − nj=1 λj Yj P and (x − m)2 1 Qn,σ2 (x) = √ exp − . σ 2π 2σ 2 Proof of Remark 5.2. Note that this condition gives us explicitly the λ0j s. We obtain therefore that (Y1 , ..., Yp , (X − X̃)) is a Gaussian vector. Moreover, we get E[(X − X̃)Yj ] = Cov(X − X̃, Yj ) = 0 and thus X − X̃ is independent of Y1 , ..., Yp . Transition Kernel and Conditional distribution Definition 6.1 (Transition Kernel). Let (E, E) and (F, F) be two measurable spaces. A transi- tion kernel from E to F is a map ν : E × F → [0, 1], such that (i) ν(x, ·) is a probability measure on F for all x ∈ E. (ii) x 7→ ν(x, A) is E-measurable for all A ∈ F. Example 6.2. Let ρ be a σ-finite measure on F and let f : E × F → R+ be a map such that Z f (x, y)dρ(y) = 1. F Then Z ν(x, A) = f (x, y)dρ(y) A is a transition kernel. An example for f would be (x − y)2 1 f (x, y) = √ exp − . σ 2π 2σ 2 Proposition 6.3. The following two hold. (i) Let h be a nonnegative (or bounded) Borel function on a measurable space (F, F). Then Z ϕ(x) = h(y)ν(x, dy) F is a nonnegative (or bounded) measurable function on a measurable space (E, E). (ii) If ρ is a probability measure on a measurable space (E, E), then Z µ(A) = ν(x, A)dρ(x), E is a probability measure on a measurable space (F, F) for all A ∈ F. Definition 6.4 (Conditional Distribution). Let X and Y be two r.v.’s with values in a mea- surable space (E, E). The conditional distribution of Y given X is any transition kernel ν from E to F such that for all nonnegative (or bounded), measurable maps h on a measurable space (F, F) one has Z E[h(Y ) | X] = h(y)ν(X, dy) a.s., F where the last equality should be understood as a map φ(X) given by Z φ : x 7→ h(y)ν(x, dy). F Remark 6.5. If ν is the conditional distribution of Y given X, we get for all A ∈ F P[Y ∈ A | X] = ν(X, A) a.s., where we have set h = 1A in the definition. If ν 0 is another such conditional distribution, we get ν(X, A) = ν 0 (X, A) a.s.  <!-- lvl=1 i=178 sid=body[1]/p[179] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

X E[|Sn |] ≤ E[|Xi |] < ∞. i=1 (ii) It is clear that Sn is Fn -measurable, since it is a function X1 , ..., Xn , which are Fn - measurable. (iii) Observe that E[Sn+1 | Fn ] = E[X1 + ... + Xn +Xn+1 | Fn ] | {z } Sn = E[Sn | Fn ] +E[Xn+1 | Fn ] = Sn + E[Xn+1 | Fn ] = Sn . | {z } Sn Therefore, (Sn )n≥0 is a martingale. Example 1.5. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space and let Y ∈ L1 (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P). Define a sequence (Xn )n≥0 by Xn := E[Y | Fn ]. Then (Xn )n≥0 is an Fn -martingale. Proof of Example 1.5. Again, we show the assumptions for a martingale. (i) Since |Xn | ≤ E[|Y | | Fn ], we get E[|Xn |] ≤ E[|Y |] < ∞. (ii) E[Y | Fn ] is Fn -measurable by definition. (iii) With the tower property, we get E[Xn+1 | Fn ] = E[E[Y | Fn+1 ] | Fn ] = E[Y | Fn ] = Xn . | {z } Xn+1 Therefore, (Xn )n≥0 is a martingale. Definition 1.6 (Regularity of Martingales). Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. A martingale (Xn )n≥0 is said to be regular, if there exists a r.v. Y ∈ L1 (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) such that Xn = E[Y | Fn ] for all n ≥ 0. Proposition 1.7. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let (Xn )n≥0 be a mar- tingale. Then the map n 7→ E[Xn ] is constant, i.e. for all n ≥ 0 E[Xn ] = E[X0 ]. Proof. By the definition of a martingale, we get E[Xn ] = E[E[Xn | F0 ]] = X0 .  <!-- lvl=1 i=185 sid=body[1]/p[186] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
- 1. DISCRETE TIME MARTINGALES 97 Definition 1.8 (Discrete Stopping time). Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. A r.v. T : Ω → N̄ = N ∪ {∞} is called a stopping time if for every n ≥ 0 {T ≤ n} ∈ Fn . Remark 1.9. Another, more general definition is used for continuous stochastic processes and may be given in terms of a filtration. Let (I, ≤) be an ordered index set (often I = [0, ∞)) or a compact subset thereof, thought of as the set of possible times), and let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Then a r.v. T : Ω → I is called a stopping time if {T ≤ t} ∈ Ft for all t ∈ I. Often, to avoid confusion, we call it a Ft -stopping time and explicitly specify the filtration. Speaking concretely, for T to be a stopping time, it should be possible to decide whether or not {T ≤ t} has occurred on the basis of the knowledge of Ft , i.e. {T ≤ t} is Ft -measurable. Proposition 1.10. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Then (i) Constant times are stopping times. (ii) The map T : (Ω, F) → (N̄, P(N̄)) is a stopping time if and only if {T ≤ n} ∈ Fn for all n ≥ 0. (iii) If S and T are stopping times, then S ∧ T , S ∨ T and S + T are also stopping times. (iv) Let (Tn )n≥0 be a sequence of stopping times. Then supn T , inf n Tn , lim inf n Tn and lim supn Tn are also stopping times. (v) Let (Xn )n≥0 be a sequence of adapted r.v.’s with values in some measure space (E, E) and let H ∈ E. Then, with the convention that inf ∅ = ∞, DH = inf{n ∈ N | Xn ∈ H} is a stopping time. Proof. We need to show all points. (i) This is clear. Theorem 1.12. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let T be a bounded stopping time and let (Xn )n≥0 be a martingale. Then we have E[XT ] = E[X0 ]. Proof. Assume that T ≤ N ∈ N. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let T be a stopping time for (Fn )n≥0 . We call the σ-Algebra of events prior of T and write FT for the σ-Algebra FT = {A ∈ F | A ∩ {T ≤ n} ∈ Fn , ∀n ≥ 0}. Remark 1.14. We need to show that FT is indeed a σ-Algebra. Proposition 1.15. If T is a stopping time, FT is a σ-Algebra. Proof. It’s clear that for a filtered probability space (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P), Ω ∈ FT . If A ∈ FT , then AC ∩ {T ≤ n} = {T ≤ n} \(A ∩ {T ≤ n}) ∈ Fn | {z } | {z } ∈Fn ∈Fn and hence AC ∈ Fn . If (Ai )i≥0 ∈ FT , then [ ∞ [ Ai ∩ {T ≤ n} = Ai ∩ {T ≤ n} . | {z } i≥0 i=1 ∈Fn S Hence i≥0 Ai ∈ FT . Therefore FT is a σ-Algebra. Remark 1.16. If T = n0 is constant, then FT = Fn0 .  <!-- lvl=1 i=189 sid=body[1]/p[190] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

L with values in N̄ is a stopping time if and only if 1{L≤n} n≥0 is (Fn )-adapted and for the case it’s a stopping time, we get L = inf{n ≥ 0 | 1{L≤n} = 1}. Proposition 1.18. Let S and T be two stopping times. (i) If S ≤ T , then FS ⊂ FT . (ii) FS∧T = FS ∩ FT . (iii) {S ≤ T }, {S = T } and {S < T } are FS ∩ FT -measurable. Proof. We need to show all three points. (i) For n ∈ N and A ∈ FS we get A ∩ {T ≤ n} = A ∩ {S ≤ n} ∩ {T ≤ n} = (A ∩ {S ≤ n}) ∩ {T ≤ n} ∈ Fn . | {z } | {z } {T ≤n} ∈Fn Therefore A ∈ FT . (ii) Since S ∧ T ≤ S, we get by (i) that FS∧T ⊂ FS and similarly that FS∧T ⊂ FT . Let now A ∈ FS ∩ FT . Then     A ∩ {S ∧ T ≤ n} =  A ∩ {S ≤ n}  ∪  A ∩ {T ≤ n}  ∈ Fn .     | {z } | {z } ∈Fn ,(since A ∈ FS ) ∈Fn ,(since A ∈ FT ) Therefore A ∈ FS∧T . (iii) Note that {S ≤ T } ∩ {T = n} = {S ≤ n} ∩ {T = n} ∈ Fn . Therefore {S ≤ T } ∈ FT . Note also that {S < T } ∩ {T = n} = {S < n} ∩ {T = n} ∈ Fn . Therefore {S < T } ∈ FT . Finally, note that {S = T } ∩ {T = n} = {S = n} ∩ {T = n} ∈ Fn . Thus {S = T } ∈ FT . Similarly one can show that these events are also FS -measurable. Proposition 1.19. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let (Xn )n≥0 be a stochastic process, which is adapted, i.e. Xn is Fn -measurable for all n ≥ 0. - 2. SUBMARTINGALES AND SUPERMARTINGALES 101 Proof. Let 0 ≤ m < n < ∞ and Λ ∈ Fm . Define for all ω ∈ Ω T (ω) = m1ΛC (ω) + n1Λ (ω). Then T is a stopping time. Therefore E[X0 ] = E[XT ] = E[Xm 1ΛC + Xn 1Λ ] = E[Xm ]. Hence we get E[Xm 1Λ ] = E[Xn 1Λ ] and thus E[Xn | Fm ] = Xm a.s. 2. Submartingales and Supermartingales Definition 2.1 (Submartingale and Supermartingale). Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered prob- ability space. A stochastic process (Xn )n≥0 is called a submartingale (resp. supermartingale) if (i) E[|Xn |] < ∞ for all n ≥ 0 (ii) (Xn )n≥0 is Fn -adapted. (iii) E[Xn | Fm ] ≥ Xm a.s. for all m ≤ n (resp. E[Xn | Fm ] ≤ Xm a.s. for all m ≤ n) Remark 2.2. A stochastic process (Xn )n≥0 is a martingale if and only if it is a submartingale and a supermartingale. A martingale is in particular a submartingale and a supermartingale. If (Xn )n≥0 is a submartingale, then the map n 7→ E[Xn ] is increasing. If (Xn )n≥0 is a super- martingale, then the map n 7→ E[Xn ] is decreasing. Example 2.3. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let Sn = nj=1 Yj , where P (Yn )n≥1 is a sequence of iid r.v.’s. Moreover, let S0 = 0, F0 = {∅, Ω} and Fn = σ(Y1 , ..., Yn ). Then we get E[Sn+1 | Fn ] = Sn + E[Yn+1 ]. If E[Yn+1 ] > 0, then E[Sn+1 | Fn ] ≥ Sn and thus (Sn )n≥0 is a submartingale. On the other hand, if E[Yn+1 ] < 0, then E[Sn+1 | Fn ] ≤ Sn and thus (Sn )n≥0 is a supermartingale. Proposition 2.4. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. If (Mn )n≥0 is a mar- tingale and ϕ is a convex function such that ϕ(Mn ) ∈ L1 (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) for all n ≥ 0, then (ϕ(Mn ))n≥0 is a submartingale. Proof. The first two conditions for a martingale are clearly satisfied. Now for m ≤ n, we get E[Mn | Fm ] = Mm a.s., since (Mn )n≥0 is assumed to be a martingale. Hence, with Jensen’s inequality, we get ϕ(E[Mn | Fm ]) = ϕ(Mm ) ≤ E[ϕ(Mn ) | Fm ] a.s. Corollary 2.5. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. If (Mn )n≥0 is a martin- gale, then (i) (|Mn |)n≥0 and (Mn+ )n≥0 are submartingales. (ii) if for all n ≥ 0, E[Mn2 ] < ∞, then (Mn2 )n≥0 is a submartingale.  <!-- lvl=1 i=195 sid=body[1]/p[196] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
- 2. SUBMARTINGALES AND SUPERMARTINGALES 103 Corollary 2.8. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let X = (Xn )n≥0 be a supermartingale. Then there exists a.s. a unique decomposition Xn = X0 + Mn − An , where M = (Mn )n≥0 is a martingale with M0 = 0 and A = (An )n≥0 is a increasing process with A0 = 0. Proof. Let Yn = −Xn for all n ≥ 0. Then the stochastic process obtained by (Yn )n≥0 is a submartingale. Theorem 8.4. tells us that there exists a unique decomposition Yn = Y0 + Ln + Cn , where Ln denotes the martingale part and Cn the increasing process part. Hence we get Xn = X0 − Ln − Cn and if we take Mn = −Ln and An = Cn , the claim follows. Now consider a stopped process. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let T be a stopping time and let (Xn )n≥0 be a stochastic process. We denote by X T = (XnT )n≥0 the process (Xn∧T )n≥0 . Proposition 2.9. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let (Xn )n≥0 be a mar- tingale (resp. sub- or supermartingale) and let T be a stopping time. Then (Xn∧T )n≥0 is also a martingale (resp. sub- or supermartingale). Proof. Note that {T ≥ n + 1} = {T ≤ n}C ∈ Fn . Hence we have E[Xn+1∧T − Xn∧T | Fn ] = E[(Xn+1∧T − Xn∧T )1{T ≥n+1} | Fn ] = 1{T ≥n+1} E[Xn+1 − Xn | Fn ]. If (Xn )n≥0 is a martingale, we deduce that E[Xn+1∧T − Xn∧T | Fn ] = 0. Moreover, Xn∧T is Fn -measurable. Therefore E[Xn+1∧T | Fn ] = Xn∧T . The same holds for sub-and super martingales. Theorem 2.10. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let (Xn )n≥0 be a sub- martingale (resp. supermartingale) and let S and T be two bounded stopping times, such that S ≤ T a.s. Then E[XT | FS ] ≥ XS a.s. (resp. E[XT | FS ] ≤ XS a.s.) Proof. Let us assume that (Xn )n≥0 is a supermartingale. Let A ∈ FS such that S ≤ T ≤ C ∈ N. We already know that (Xn∧T )n≥0 is a supermartingale. Therefore we get  <!-- lvl=1 i=199 sid=body[1]/p[200] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

- 7. STOPPING THEOREMS 115 7. Stopping theorems If S ≤ T are two bounded stopping times and (Xn )n≥0 a martingale, then E[XT | FS ] = XS a.s. If (Xn )n≥0 is an adapted process, which converges a.s. to X∞ , we can define XT for all stopping times (finite or not) by ∞ X XT = Xn 1{T =n} + X∞ 1{T =∞} . n=0 Theorem 7.1. Let (Ω, F, (Fn )n≥0 , P) be a filtered probability space. Let (Xn )n≥0 be u.i. mar- tingale. Then for any stopping time T , we have that E[X∞ | FT ] = XT a.s. In particular E[XT ] = E[X∞ ] = E[Xn ] for all n ≥ 0. If S and T are two stopping times, such that S ≤ T , then E[XT | FS ] = XS a.s. Proof. - 2. THE CANONICAL MARKOV CHAIN 131 which contains all the sets of the form Xn−1 (y) for y ∈ E. Hence all the Xn ’s are measurable with respect to A and the claim follows. Theorem 2.3. Let (Ω, F, P) be a probability space. Let Q be a stochastic matrix on E. For all x ∈ E, there exists a unique probability measure Px , on Ω = E N , such that the sequence of coordinate functions (Xn )n≥0 is a Markov chain with transition matrix Q and Px [X0 = x] = 1. Proof. From proposition 2.1, there exists (Ω0 , F 0 , P0 ) and (Xnx )n≥0 which is a Markov chain with transition matrix Q and such that X0x = x. We then define Px as the image of P0 by the map Ω0 −→ Ω ω 0 7−→ (Xnx (ω 0 ))n≥0 This map is measurable because of lemma 2.2. We have Px [X0 = x] = P0 [X0x = x] = 1 and for x0 , x1 , ..., xn ∈ E we have Px [X0 = x0 , X1 = x1 , ..., Xn = xn ] = P0 [X0x = x0 , X1x = x1 , ..., Xnx = xn ] n Y Y 0 = P [X0x = x0 ] Q(xj−1 , xj ) = Px [X0 = x0 ] Q(xj−1 , xj ) j=1 j=1 Therefore it follows that the sequence of the coordinate functions is a Markov chain. For unique- ness, we note that if P0x is another such probability measure, Px and P0x coincide on cylinders. It then follows from an application of the monotone class theorem that Px = P0x . Remark 2.4. It follows for all n ≥ 0 and for all x, y ∈ E, that we have Px [Xn = y] = Qn (x, y). Remark 2.5. If µ is a probability measure on E, we write X Pµ := µ(x)Px , x∈E which defines a new probability measure on Ω. By writing an explicit formula for Pµ [X0 = x0 , ..., Xn = xn ], one immediately checks that under Pµ we get that (Xn )n≥0 is a Markov chain with transition matrix Q, where X0 has the distribution µ. Remark 2.6. Let (Xn0 )n≥0 be a Markov chain with transition matrix Q and initial distribution µ. Then for all measurable subsets B ⊂ Ω = E N , we have P[(Xn0 )n≥0 ∈ B] = Pµ [B]. This is only true when B is a cylinder as in the proof above. This shows that all results proven for the canonical Markov chains are true for a general Markov chain with the same transition matrix. A chain is called irreducible if U (x, y) > 0 for all x, y ∈ E. Corollary 3.9. If the chain is irreducible, then the following hold. If we have a norm k · k on X, and we then consider a normed space (X, k · k), we get the relation d(x, y) := kx − yk, which defines a distance. If (X, d) is a metric space, the topology on X is associated with d and is, for some arbitrary index set I, given by ( ) [ d OX := Bri (xi ) | xi ∈ X, ri ∈ R+ , Bri (xi ) := {y ∈ X | d(xi , y) < ri } i∈I Definition 2.4 (Basis and separability). A topological space (X, O) is said to have a countable basis of open sets {wn }n∈N if for every open set O ∈ O, there exists a countable index set I ⊂ N, such that [ O= wn . n∈I Moreover, a metric space (X, d) is said to be separable, if it contains a sequence (xn )n∈N which is dense in X, that is, for all x ∈ X there exists a subsequence (xnk )k∈N of (xn )n∈N , such that k→∞ d(x, xnk ) −−−→ 0. Let (X, OX ) and (Y, OY ) be two topological spaces. The product topology for the product space X × Y is defined, with an arbitrary index set I, by the family of open sets ( ) [ OX×Y := OiX × OiY | OX ∈ OX , OY ∈ OY . i∈I Definition 2.7 (Continuity). Let (X, OX ) and (Y, OY ) be two topological spaces. A map f : (X, OX ) −→ (Y, OY ) is continuous if and only if for all OY ∈ OY , the image of OY under f −1 is open, that is f −1 (OY ) = {x ∈ X | f (x) ∈ OY } ∈ OX Definition 2.8 (Canonical projection). There exists a unique measure on the measurable space (R, B(R)), which is denoted by λ, such that for all open intervals (a, b) ∈ B(R) it is given by λ((a, b)) = b − a. Definition 4.6 (Finite-, σ-finite- and probability measures). Let (E, A) be a measurable space. We say that a measure µ is (i) finite if µ(E) < ∞. (ii) a probability measure if µ(E) = 1. (iii) σ-finite if thereSexists an increasing sequence (partition of the total space) (En )n∈N ⊂ A, such that E = n∈N En and with µ(En ) < ∞ for all n ∈ N. Definition 4.7 (Atom). Let (E, A, µ) be a measure space. An element x ∈ E is called an atom for µ if the set {x} ∈ A and µ({x}) > 0. Definition 4.8 (Product σ-Algebra). Let (E1 , A1 ) and (E2 , A2 ) be two measurable spaces. Then we can define the product σ-Algebra A1 ⊗ A2 on the product space E1 × E2 by A1 ⊗ A2 = σ(A1 × A2 ), where A1 ∈ A1 and A2 ∈ A2 . This is actually the σ-Algebra which contains all sets of the form (A1 × A2 ). Let (E, A) and (F, B) be two measure spaces. Consider a map f : E −→ F . - 5. MEASURABLE MAPS 151 with the metric δ. Thus we can define the Borel σ-Algebra of R̄ by the Borel sets, which are described by the metric topology of R̄. This concept is important as we will work many times with the space R̄. Remark 4.18. It is useful to note that R̄ describes a totally ordered set, since ≤ arises with the usual naturalness as in R. Moreover, the identity map id : (R, δ |R ) −→ (R, k · k) x 7−→ x is a homeomorphism. Another useful observation is that (R̄, δ) is a compact space and homeo- morphic to the interval [−1, 1] and eventually R is an open subset of R̄. Exercise 4.19. Show that B(R̄) = σ({[a, ∞]}a∈Q ) = σ({(a, ∞]}a∈Q ). 5. Measurable Maps Measure theory and the notion of integration require special structures on functions, which need to satisfy different properties, such as being measurable or bounded. The notion of a measurable map is important for the study of the integration with respect to a certain measure and the fact that we can only consider integration with respect to a measure if the integrating function satisfies measurability. It is now important to use the σ-Algebras of the underlying spaces similar to the topological notion of continuity where the topologies of the underlying spaces are used. Let us therefore define a measurable map. Definition 5.1 (Measurable Map). Let (E, A) and (F, B) be two measurable spaces and let f : E −→ F be a map. We say that f is measurable, if for all B ∈ B we get f −1 (B) ∈ A. Proposition 5.2. Let (X, A), (Y, B) and (Z, G) be measurable space and consider the composi- tion f g (X, A) −→ (X, B) →− (Z, G). If f and g are both measurable, then g ◦ f is also measurable. Proof. Exercise.2 Proposition 5.3. Let (E, A) and (F, B) be two measurable spaces and let f : E −→ F be a map. Moreover, assume that there exists C ⊂ P(F ) such that σ(C) = B. Then f is measurable if and only if for all C ∈ C we have f −1 (C) ∈ A. Proof. Let us first define the σ-Algebra G by G := {B ∈ B | f −1 (B) ∈ A} ⊃ C. Now, since G is a σ-Algebra, we get that σ(C) ⊂ G and thus G = B, which proves the claim. Example 5.4. Let3 (F, B) = (R, B(R)). To show that f is measurable, it is enough to show either that f −1 ((a, b)) ∈ A or f −1 ((−∞, a)) ∈ A, for a, b ∈ R with a < b. Example 5.5 (continuous maps are measurable). Assume that E and F are two metric spaces (or topological spaces), endowed with their Borel σ-Algebra respectively. Then f is measurable if for every open set O of F we have f −1 (O) ∈ B(E). In particular we can say that continuous maps are measurable maps. 2Use the definition of a measurable map. 3We can also take (R̄, B(R̄)).  <!-- lvl=1 i=294 sid=body[1]/p[295] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Moreover, let f : E −→ F be a measurable map. Then the push-forward of the measure µ by f , denoted by f∗ µ is defined for all B ∈ B as f∗ µ(B) := µ(f −1 (B)). 8. Simple Functions After we have developed the notion of a measurable map, we need to discuss a class of very powerful and, as the name points out, simple functions. The advantage of these type of functions are exactly the fact that they are simple to handle and moreover one can basically proof many things for measurable functions by proving it for simple functions and deduce the general case out of that. We will later see the advantage of them being dense in different spaces. Let us start with the definition of a simple function.  <!-- lvl=1 i=302 sid=body[1]/p[303] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

If we have a function f : E −→ R+ and we assume that there exists aome M ∈ (0, ∞) such that 0 ≤ fn (x) ≤ M for all x ∈ E, then for n > M with {f ≥ n} = ∅ it follows that for all x ∈ E we get 0 ≤ f (x) − fn (x) ≤ 2−n , which implies that n→∞ sup |f (x) − fn (x)| ≤ 2−n −−−→ 0. x∈E Let us emphasize the real case. If we have a function f : E −→ R we have the decompositions as f = f + − f −, |f | = f 1 + f − , with f + , f − ≥ 0. Moreover, we notice that for x ∈ E the sequences (fn+ (x))n∈N and (fn− (x))n∈N cannot be simultaneously nonzero and therefore n→∞ fn = fn+ − fn− −−−→ f = f + − f − . 9. Monotone classes A very important notion is that of a monotone class. We will see that there are many things which can be deduced by using the monotone class lemma. Definition 9.1 (Monotone Class). Let E be some topological space and let M ⊂ P(E). M is called a monotone class if the following holds. (i) E ∈ M . (ii) Let A ∈ M and B ∈ M . If A ⊂ B =⇒SB \ A ∈ M . (iii) Let (An )n∈N ∈ M . If An ⊂ An+1 =⇒ n∈N An ∈ M . Remark 9.2. A σ-Algebra is a monotone class4 4Prove that as an exercise.  <!-- lvl=1 i=306 sid=body[1]/p[307] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

On the other hand we know that C is stable under finite intersections and therefore it follows that M (C) = σ(C) and that G = σ(C) = A. Now define for all n ∈ N and A ∈ A the two sequences µn (A) = µ(A ∩ En ) νn (A) = ν(A ∩ En ) Now since µ(E) = ν(E), we get the same for the sequence elements and obtain therefore that µn = νn . Moreover, for A ∈ A, we have µ(A) = lim µ(A ∩ En ) = lim ↑ ν(A ∩ En ) = ν(A). n→∞ n→∞ Hence we get that µ = ν. Remark 9.6. There are several applications of this corollary. Let us emphasize a first one, by giving already a small introduction to the Lebesgue measure. Assume that λ is a measure on the measurable space (R, B(R)) such that λ((a, b)) = b − a for a < b and let C be the class of intervals En = (−n, n) for n ≥ 1. With the corollary above, it follows that λ is unique. We will call λ the Lebesgue measure. A second application is that a finite measure on (R, B(R)) is uniquely characterized, for a ∈ R, by the values µ((−∞, a]) = µ((−∞, a)).  <!-- lvl=1 i=310 sid=body[1]/p[311] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->

Integrable functions Definition 2.1 (Integrable). Let (E, A, µ) be a measure space and let f : E −→ R be a mea- surable map. We say that f is integrable with respect to µ if Z |f |dµ < ∞. Moreover, if f is integrable, we define its integral to be given by Z Z Z f dµ = f dµ − f − dµ. + Remark 2.2. It always holds that Z Z ± f dµ ≤ |f |dµ. (i) If f ∈ L1 (E, A, µ), then R R f dµ ≤ |f |dµ. (ii) L1 (E, A, µ) is a vector space and the Rmap f 7→R |f |dµ is a linear form. R (iii) If f, g ∈ L1 (E, A, µ) and f ≤ g, then f dµR ≤ gdµ. - 3. LEBESGUE’S DOMINATED CONVERGENCE THEOREM 169 2.1. Extension to the complex case. Let (E, A, µ) be a measure space and let f : E −→ C(∼ = R2 ) be a measurable map, which basically means that Re(f ) and Im(f ) are both measurable maps. We say that f is integrable if Re(f ) and Im(f ) are both integrable, or equivalently Z |f |dµ < ∞, and we write f ∈ L1C (E, A, µ). This is simply because of the fact that Z Z Z Z f dµ = (Re(f ) + Im(f ))dµ = Re(f )dµ + Im(f )dµ. Moreover, the properties (i), (ii) and (iv) of proposition also hold for the complex case. 3. Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem An important question of integration theory is whether the interchanging of limits and integrals is actually possible and under which condition on the sequence of maps on some measure space. We have already seen the monotone convergence theorem and Fatou’s lemma, giving some simple conditions for such an interchange. Another way of achieving the same with different conditions is due to Lebesgue, who gave a more general condition, which is going to be discussed now. Theorem 3.1 (Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem). Let (E, A, µ) be a measure space and let (fn )n∈N be a sequence of functions in L1 (E, A, µ) (resp. L1C (E, A, µ)). Moreover, assume that the following hold. (i) There exists a measurable map f on E with values in R (resp. C) such that for all x ∈ E lim fn (x) = f (x). n→∞ (ii) There exists a psotive and measurable map g : E −→ R such that Z gdµ < ∞ and such that |fn | ≤ g a.e. for all n ∈ N. Then f ∈ L1 (E, A, µ) (resp. L1C (E, A, µ)) and we have Z lim |fn − f |dµ = 0 n→∞ and hence Z Z lim fn dµ = f dµ. n→∞ Proof. Let us first assume some stronger assumptions. (i) limn→∞ fn (x) = f (x) for all x ∈ E. (ii) There exists a positive and measurable map g : E −→ R such that Z gdµ < ∞ and |fn (x)| ≥ g(x) for all x ∈ E. Consider the general case where (i) and (ii) hold. Now define the set n o n→∞ A = x ∈ E | fn (x) −−−→ f (x) and ∀n, |fn (x)| ≤ g(x) . Then µ(AC ) = 0. Let us now apply the first part of the proof to f˜n (x) = 1A (x)fn (x) f˜(x) = 1A (x)f (x). Thus we have that f = f˜ a.e. and fn = f˜n a.e. - 5. DIFFERENTIATION OF PARAMETER INTEGRALS 171 Theorem 4.2. Let (E, A, µ) be a measure space. Let (U, d) be a metric space and let f : R U × E −→ with u0 ∈ U . Moreover, assume that the following hold. C (i) The map x 7→ f (u, x) is measurable for all x ∈ U . (ii) The map u 7→ f (u, x) is continuous at u0 a.e. (iii) There exists a measurable function g ∈ L1 (E, A, µ) such that for all u ∈ U |f (u, x)| ≤ g(x) -a.e. R Then the map F (u) = E f (u, x)dµ is well defined and continuous at u0 . Proof. From (iii) follows that the map x 7→ f (u, x) is integrable for every u ∈ U , and n→∞ so F (u) is well defined. Take a sequence (un )n∈N ∈ U such that un −−−→ u0 , which basically n→∞ n→∞ means that d(un , u0 ) −−−→ 0. Then by continuity from (ii), we get that f (un , x) −−−→ f (u0 , x) a.e. and from (iii) we can apply Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem to obtain that Z Z lim F (un ) = lim f (un , x)dµ = f (u0 , x)dµ = F (u0 ). n→∞ n→∞ Remark 4.3. Observe that F (u) is continuous if F is continuous at every point u ∈ U . Example 4.4 (Fourier analysis). we want to give several examples of Fourier analysis at this point. (1) Let µ be a measure on (R, B(R)) such that µ({x}) = 0 for all x ∈ R. Moreover, let φ ∈ L1 (R, B(R), µ). Then the map Z Z F (u) = 1(−∞,u] (x)φ(x)dµ = φ(x)dµ R (−∞,u] is continuous. Here we have f (u, x) = 1(−∞,u] (x)φ(x). The map u 7→ f (u, x) is continuous at u0 for all x ∈ R \ {u0 } for some u0 ∈ R. But µ({u0 }) = 0, which implies that the map u 7→ f (u, x) is a.e. continuous at u0 with |f (u, x)| ≤ φ(x)| and |φ| is integrable by assumption. (2) Consider now the Lebesgue measure λ and φ ∈ L1 (R, B(R), λ). Define moreover Z φ̂(u) = eiux φ(x)dλ, R which is called the Fourier-transform of φ. The map u 7→ eiux φ(x) is actually continuous for all x ∈ R, which implies that φ̂(u) is continuous at any u ∈ R. (3) Let again φ ∈ L1 (R, B(R), λ) and h : R −→ R be a continuous and bounded map. The convolution of h and φ is given by Z (h ∗ φ)(u) = h(u − x)φ(x)dλ(x). R The map (h ∗ φ) is continuous at any u ∈ R. Moreover, the map u 7→ h(u − x)φ(x) is continuous for all x ∈ R. Since h is bounded, there exists a constant k > 0, such that |h(x)| ≤ k for all x ∈ R. 5. Differentiation of Parameter Integrals Theorem 5.1 (Differentiation of Integrals). Let I ∈ R be a real interval and (E, A, µ) a measure R space. Let f : I × E −→ and let u0 ∈ I. Moreover, assume that the following hold. C (i) The map x 7→ f (u, x) is in L1 (E, A, µ) for all u ∈ I. (ii) The map u 7→ f (u, x) is a.e. differentiable at u0 with derivation denoted by ∂u f (u0 , x). (iii) There exists a map g ∈ L1 (E, A, µ) such that for all u ∈ I |f (u, x) − f (u0 , x)| ≤ g(x)|u − u0 |.  <!-- lvl=1 i=333 sid=body[1]/p[334] src=Structure CẦN-XEM-LẠI -->
